# FILE: ALGORITHMS.md

# Proposed algorithms: baselines, splits, metrics and rules (fixed before any result was looked at)

Written 2026-11-11, before the algorithm runs. Rules that apply to all three (from CLAUDE.md and the author's instruction):

- A claim counts only if the improvement over its baseline clears the 95% bootstrap confidence interval (1,000 resamples, seed 42) on data the method was not tuned on. Otherwise the result is reported as **no improvement found**, in the same detail as a success. A non-result is never presented as a contribution.
- Nothing is tuned on a test split. Every parameter below is chosen on a tuning part; the held-out part is scored once.
- `TRIGGER_G`, `MPU_RANGE_G`, `PRE_S` and `POST_S` stay placeholders in `config.py`; every system-level result is a function of them.
- Every number goes to `results/results.json` with an origin label. The banned words listed in CLAUDE.md are not used.
- Status words in the novelty files: SUPPORTED (clears the interval on data not tuned on), PARTIAL, NO_IMPROVEMENT_FOUND.

---

## N14. Saturation-aware severity estimator

**Problem.** A 16 g accelerometer clips real crash pulses (the NHTSA tests peak at about 42 g after the front-end filter), so plain delta-V from the clipped signal is biased low. The range study shows the fix by hardware (a wider range). This algorithm asks whether software can repair the clipped signal.

**Data.** The NHTSA tests of `results/tables/sevstudy_tests.csv`: frontal barrier tests and side impactor tests, vehicle-CG or front-sill accelerometer. Split by vehicle group (make, model, model year), fixed on 2026-11-11 (docs/07_log/DECISIONS.md, follow-up 1): groups sorted by their smallest test number, ranks 3, 6, 9, ... are HELD-OUT with all their tests, the others are TUNING; no group spans both sides (an earlier version split by test number). Leave-one-test-out cross-validation (LOTO) on the tuning set chooses every parameter; the held-out tests are scored once. Synthetic pulses with known delta-V are a second, SIMULATED data set.

**Reference.** The reference delta-V of `severity_study.reference_dv`: the largest resultant of the running integral of the original-rate, unclipped, baseline-removed horizontal acceleration within 300 ms of the trigger. Error is the sensor-model estimate against this reference.

**Sensor model.** The generic model of `severity_study.sensor_signal`: 260 Hz front end (4th-order zero-phase low-pass), saturation at the range, a digital low-pass at 0.4 x the sample rate, sampling at 100 Hz. Ranges +-16 g, +-32 g and +-64 g.

**Baseline B.** Plain delta-V integration of the clipped 100 Hz signal with the current `severity.py` (release rule 0.1 x peak, floor 0.5 g, 50 ms hold, 250 ms cap), as already implemented.

**Method M (saturation-aware).** Applied only when the pulse is saturated; otherwise M equals B.
1. *Saturation test.* A sample is saturated when |a_x| or |a_y| (the sensor reading, gravity included) is at least 0.98 x range. The pulse is the interval the baseline pulse rule gives (trigger to release). If no sample of the pulse is saturated, M = B.
2. *Pulse model.* The horizontal resultant h(t) = sqrt(a_x^2 + a_y^2) of the baseline-removed signal is modelled as A x s((t - t0) / T) for t0 <= t <= t0 + T and 0 elsewhere, with s one of: half-sine `sin(pi u)`, haversine `(1 - cos(2 pi u)) / 2`, triangle `1 - |2u - 1|` (u in [0, 1]).
3. *Fit.* A deterministic grid search, so that the same rule can be written in C without heap: t0 in {start - 1, start - 0.75, ..., start + 1} samples (9 values; start = the trigger sample), T in 21 values from 0.6 to 1.6 x the baseline pulse length (equal steps). For each (t0, T) the amplitude A is the least-squares amplitude over the UNSATURATED samples of the pulse, raised to at least the saturation level L = 0.98 x range (the clipped samples must be at or above the limit). The cost is the sum of squared errors on the unsaturated samples plus the sum of squared shortfalls max(0, L - A s) over the saturated samples. The (t0, T, A) with the lowest cost is kept. This uses the pulse timing (the unsaturated rise and fall) and the level of the unsaturated part.
4. *Fill.* At each saturated sample the horizontal vector is rescaled so that its magnitude is max(h_measured, A s(t)); the direction is kept. The baseline severity code is then run on the filled window, so delta-V and ASI both change.
5. *Family choice.* Half-sine is the primary method. Haversine and triangle are tried; the family with the lowest LOTO median absolute error on the tuning tests at +-16 g is the reported family (ties go to half-sine). The choice is made on the tuning tests only.

**Method M+ (learned correction).** On top of M, a ridge regression predicts the log of (reference / M estimate) from three inputs: the delta-V of the unsaturated part (km/h), the saturated share of the pulse samples, and the pulse width (ms). Inputs are standardised on the tuning set; the regularisation strength is chosen from {0.01, 0.1, 1, 10, 100} by LOTO on the tuning set. The correction is applied only when the pulse is saturated. One model per range.

**Metrics** (each at +-16, +-32 and +-64 g, for B, M and M+): median and 90th percentile of the absolute delta-V error (%), the share of tests within +-10% and within +-20% of the reference, and the share of frontal tests inside v <= delta-V <= 2 v (v = test impact speed). Each with a 95% bootstrap interval over tests. The comparison with the baseline is paired on the same tests: the difference in median absolute error and in the share within +-10%, with a 95% interval. Failure cases are all tests where the method's absolute error is larger than the baseline's.

**Decision rule (primary endpoint).** The difference (M or M+ minus B) in the median absolute delta-V error on the HELD-OUT tests at +-16 g. The method is SUPPORTED at a range only if the whole 95% interval of that difference is below zero. The same test is applied at +-32 g and +-64 g. The share within +-10% is secondary.

**Synthetic check (SIMULATED).** Half-sine, haversine, triangular and (as a mismatched case) square pulses with analytic delta-V, peak 20 to 150 g, duration 40 to 150 ms, passed through the same sensor model. Errors of B and M against the analytic value.

**C version.** The saturation test, the grid fit for the chosen family, the fill and (if M+ is kept) the correction are added to `firmware_export/severity.h` and checked against Python on the NHTSA and synthetic windows. Flash and RAM are host estimates (ANALYTICAL).

**Prior work to search:** clipped or saturated accelerometer reconstruction and pulse-function fits in crash and impact analysis. Queries, dates and closest work go to `docs/06_references/PRIOR_WORK.md`.

---

## N15. Trigger-aware training

**Problem.** The detector is a second stage behind a fixed trigger. This asks whether choosing or learning the trigger together with the classifier raises the system recall at a fixed false-alarm rate. **VZCrash events were already selected on the device by threshold heuristics, so the trigger here is a second stage; the results do not apply to continuous driving.**

**Data.** The de-duplicated VZCrash splits (primary test set), the same events and windows as the existing results. Thresholds and every choice below use the validation split only; the test split is scored once per method and seed.

**Baseline B.** The fixed trigger (config value, 2.0 g) followed by a separately trained 15-tree, depth-6 forest (the deployed model), with the decision threshold chosen on validation to meet the target false-alarm rate. Seeds 42 (headline) and 0 to 4 (mean and standard deviation).

**Target false-alarm rates.** 1% and 5% of ALL non-crash events (system level: events the trigger discards count as correct negatives). The threshold is the smallest one for which the validation false-alarm rate does not exceed the target.

**Method a. Joint selection.** For each trigger level in {1.5, 2.0, 2.5, 3.0} g (the variants already extracted), train the forest on the triggered training events, choose the threshold on validation for the target rate, and score validation system recall. The trigger level with the highest validation system recall at the target rate is selected (ties go to the config trigger). The test set is scored once for that selection.

**Method b. Cost-sensitive training.** Crashes that the config trigger discards are treated as errors: their peak-centred window features (the existing `peak` variant) are added to the training set as positives with weight g, for g in {0, 0.5, 1, 2}; g = 0 is the baseline. The weight is chosen on validation (highest system recall at the target rate). The test events are triggered with the config trigger as in the baseline.

**Method c. Learned trigger.** A rule on three cheap running features that can be computed without buffering the window: the running peak |a| (p), the jerk (largest absolute difference of |a| between consecutive samples, per second, j) and the energy of the last 10 samples (sum of (|a| - 1)^2 times the sample period, e). The trigger fires at the earliest sample where `|a| >= p`, or where `jerk >= j AND energy >= e`. Candidates: p in {2.0, 2.5, 3.0, 4.0} g, j in {50, 100, 200} g/s (j off = infinity), e in {0.5, 1.0, 2.0} g^2 s. A candidate is a combination of the three; the total is at most 4 x 4 x 3 = 48 candidates (j off needs no e). For every candidate the windows, features and a forest are rebuilt and the validation system recall at the target rate is computed; the best candidate is selected on validation. RAM and operation counts are ANALYTICAL.

**Metrics.** System recall at 1% and 5% false-alarm rate (test), the area under the system-level precision-recall curve (system AP), the trigger rate by VZCrash vehicle-size category (crash events), all with 5 seeds mean +- std. The comparison with B is the paired bootstrap difference on the same test events (1,000 resamples): the difference in system recall at each target, and in system AP.

**Decision rule.** A method is SUPPORTED only if the whole 95% interval of its paired difference in system recall (at either target rate, stated which) is above zero on the test set. Otherwise NO_IMPROVEMENT_FOUND.

**Prior work to search:** joint trigger and classifier design, cascade detectors, cost-sensitive early-stage rejection in crash or impact detection.

---

## N16. Severity- and budget-aware delivery scheduler (supporting evidence only)

**Problem.** Under a 10% duty cycle, which alerts get airtime earlier, and how should retries be spaced? Everything here is SIMULATED and is supporting evidence that hardware test H3 would replace.

**Baselines.** (i) the fixed 3-retry policy (arrival order, immediate retry); (ii) the current severity-table policy with the token bucket (maximum tries 2, 4, 8 and 12 for low, moderate, severe and critical, the most severe waiting alert next, immediate retry).

**Scheduler S (all rules and parameters).**
1. *Reserve.* A fraction `rho` of the node's token capacity (360 s) is kept for critical alerts: a non-critical alert may be sent only if tokens after the send are at least `rho` x capacity.
2. *Order.* Waiting alerts are served by effective severity = class + min(age / tau, 0.99), highest served next, with `tau` = 300 s (an old low alert can overtake a newer one of the same class but never one of a higher class).
3. *Retry spacing.* After the k-th failed try the next try is ready after the listening window plus `m x base_sev x 2^min(k-1, 5) x (1 + jitter x u) x (1 + 2 x (1 - p_hat))`, capped at `cap_sev`, where u is uniform in [0, 1), `p_hat` is an exponentially weighted share of successful tries (weight 0.3, start 1.0, updated after each try by ACK feedback), `base_sev` = 0.5, 1, 2 and 4 s and `cap_sev` = 8, 16, 32 and 64 s for critical, severe, moderate and low. Maximum tries are those of baseline (ii).
4. *Spreading factor.* If enabled, after 3 consecutive failures of an alert of class moderate or above the alert's next try uses SF + 1 (at most SF12), but only if the tokens after the send are at least twice the new airtime. ASSUMPTIONS stated in the report: the base receives all spreading factors, and the per-transmission loss at SF s is the SF7 loss times 0.7 per step above SF7 (no measurement; H3 would measure it).
5. *Parameters to tune:* `rho` in {0, 0.2}, `m` in {0.5, 1, 2}, `jitter` in {0, 0.5}, SF raise on or off (24 combinations).

**Scenarios.** Tuning scenarios: seeds 100 to 104, iid loss 15%, 35% and 55%, a Gilbert-Elliott burst at mean 25% and 45% (P_BG = 0.3, bad-state loss 0.9), SF7 and SF12, event rates 20 and 120 per hour, the base at its full budget or with 2 s left. Held-out scenarios (different seeds 200 to 204 and different loss settings, never used for tuning): iid loss 10%, 30%, 50% and 60%, burst at mean 30% and 50%, separate ACK loss (uplink 30%, downlink 10% and 50%), event rates 10, 60 and 240 per hour, a base already near its budget (2 s left), SF7, SF9 and SF12.

**Tuning objective (fixed now).** J = mean over tuning scenarios of [ (1 x delivered_low + 2 x delivered_moderate + 4 x delivered_severe + 8 x delivered_critical) / 15 - 0.2 x airtime_share_total ], where delivered_c is the share of class-c alerts whose ACK reached the car; a class with no alerts in a scenario is left out of that scenario's weighted sum and the weights are renormalised. The best combination is the one with the highest J; ties go to fewer enabled features.

**Severity classes of the alerts.** From the replay pipeline (docs/02_preregistration/ALGORITHMS.md sections above and `replay.py`): the real VZCrash mix (almost all low), the NHTSA mix through the 16 g sensor with the plain estimator, the NHTSA mix with the saturation-aware estimator (N14), the reference classes, and an assumed 40/30/20/10 stress mix. For the estimator comparison each alert has a TRUE class (reference delta-V) and an ESTIMATED class (what the car would send); the policy uses the estimated class and the results are reported by the true class.

**Metrics** for each class: delivery rate (ACK reached the car), median and 95th percentile of the trigger-to-ACK time, mean retries, share of the duty-cycle budget used, and the number of critical alerts blocked by budget exhaustion (initial transmission delayed by more than 1 s, and never sent within the hour). Bootstrap 95% intervals over simulated hours (scenario seeds x hours); policy comparisons are paired on the same arrivals.

**Decision rule.** N16 is supporting evidence, never the headline. It is reported SUPPORTED for a conclusion only where the paired 95% interval of the held-out difference excludes zero; the report lists which conclusions survive when the loss model, spreading factor, event rate, cancel window or base budget changes, and which do not.

## Order of work and files
N14: `src/crashguard/saturation.py` (+ tests, + C in `severity.h`); N15: `src/crashguard/trigger_aware.py` (+ tests); N16: `src/crashguard/scheduler.py` (+ tests). Results go to `results/results.json` under `n14.*`, `n15.*` and `n16.*`; figures and tables under `results/`.

## Revision log (kept in the open; no held-out result had been looked at at the time of any entry)

**2026-11-11, N14, development on the TUNING tests only.** An initial run of the method as written (saturation level 0.98 x range, three pulse families) on the 118 tuning tests at 100 Hz showed that the fill barely changes delta-V (median absolute error at +-16 g: baseline 25.3%, fill 23 to 25%). Reason: in the sensor chain the clipping happens before the 40 Hz low-pass, so the clipped plateau is smoothed and few samples reach 0.98 x range. The saturation level is therefore a parameter of the method, chosen with the pulse family on the tuning tests: levels {0.98, 0.9, 0.8, 0.7, 0.6, 0.5} x range, families {half-sine, haversine, triangle}; the pair with the lowest mean over the three ranges of the median absolute error on the tuning tests is used (ties: half-sine, then the higher level). Samples at or above the level are treated as lower bounds (they may be filled but are not used in the fit); the fit uses the samples below the level (the rise and fall). Nothing else changed. The held-out tests were not used in this development; they are scored once by `saturation_study.py`.

**2026-11-11, N15 decision rule made explicit after the initial run (this one tightens, not loosens, the rule).** The initial run printed the seed-42 paired differences: for method a (trigger 3.0 g chosen on validation at the 1% target) recall was +1.4 points with an interval above zero, while the mean over seeds 0 to 4 was -0.2 points (baseline 0.6265 +- 0.0039, method 0.6247 +- 0.0050). A single model draw is not enough, so the decision rule uses the SEED-AVERAGED paired difference: for each bootstrap resample of test events, the mean over seeds 0 to 4 of (method recall - baseline recall). A method is SUPPORTED only if the whole 95% interval of that difference is above zero (system recall at the stated target); the seed-42 difference and the change in system AP are reported next to it. Nothing else changed.

---

## N14 extension (Part 0B, B1). Definitions written 2026-11-11 BEFORE the B1 code was run

**Why.** The closest threat to N14 is spline restoration of clipped acceleration (Ruder et al., applied and tested by Chan et al. 2023, Sensors 23(10) 4609; opened through Europe PMC, PMC10222349). N14 is compared with it, on stricter splits, before any claim is kept. **Status of the earlier result:** the grouped held-out set (58 tests, 56 groups; tuning 118 tests, 112 groups) was seen in the earlier N14 and release-rule results, so a rerun on it is "stable under re-splitting", not a blind test. No result here is described as a blind test.

**Primary method.** The earlier text above names "M or M+" in the decision rule and says half-sine is the primary pulse family; it never names one of fill-only (M) or fill + ridge (M+) as primary. That is stated openly. From this point (set now, before the B1 run, so after the earlier numbers were seen) the primary method for B1 is **M+ (fill + ridge)**, because the author's decision rule is written for it. M (fill only) is reported next to it. Nothing about M+ is changed.

**Methods compared (four), each at +-16, +-32 and +-64 g, on the same sensor model as above:**
1. **Plain** (B): integration of the clipped signal.
2. **Spline restoration** (SP), fixed as follows. Per horizontal axis (x and y) of the sensor reading, a *clipped segment* is a maximal run of consecutive samples, from the trigger sample to the end of the baseline pulse (the same pulse end as B), with |reading| >= L x range. L is the saturation level, chosen on the original TUNING tests from {0.98, 0.9, 0.8, 0.7, 0.6, 0.5} by the lowest mean over the three ranges of the median absolute error (the same selection rule as M; ties go to the higher level). For each segment the 3 samples immediately before it and the 3 immediately after it (clean samples: below L x range, not in another segment) are taken; a 5th-order spline (order 5, as in the MATLAB `spapi(5, ...)` call that Chan et al. report; implemented as degree 4 with `scipy.interpolate.make_interp_spline`, so knots may differ from MATLAB) is interpolated through these 6 points and evaluated at the segment samples. The restored value replaces the measured one only where its magnitude is larger (restoration may not shrink the reading) and is capped at 12 x range (same cap as M). **If fewer than 3 clean samples exist on either side (window edge, or another segment closer than 3 samples), that segment is left as measured.** Delta-V is then computed from the restored window with the same severity code.
3. **Fill** (M) and 4. **fill + ridge** (M+) exactly as defined above (triangle pulse, saturation level 0.7, release fraction 0.1, ridge strength chosen by leave-one-test-out on the tuning tests of the split being used).

**Metrics per method, range and split:** median and 90th percentile of the absolute delta-V error (%), share within +-10% and within +-20%. **Paired comparisons** (M+ minus SP, M minus SP, M+ minus plain, SP minus plain): difference in median absolute error and in share within +-10%, 95% bootstrap interval **resampled by vehicle group** (1,000 resamples, seed 42; all tests of a drawn group are drawn together).

**Decision rule (fixed now).** N14 counts as an improvement at a range, on a split, only if the whole interval of (M+ minus SP) in median absolute error is below zero AND the whole interval of (M+ minus plain) is below zero. Where SP matches M+ (interval contains zero) or is better, the result is reported that way and N14 is re-worded in `novelty_summary.md` as "a comparison of clipping-restoration methods for delta-V at low sensor range", with the pulse-fit method labelled as not better than spline restoration. Where M+ is not better than plain at +-32 g and +-64 g the words are "no improvement found".

**Splits.**
- **O. Original test-number split.** Within each kind (frontal, side), tests sorted by test number; ranks 3, 6, 9, ... held out.
- **G. Grouped by make, model and model year** (the current split; groups sorted by smallest test number, every third held out).
- **A. Stricter split A: grouped by make and model only** (model year ignored); groups sorted by their smallest test number, every third group held out. The numbers of groups and tests on each side are reported.
- **R. Repeated grouped cross-validation** over all groups (make and model), 5 folds x 10 repeats, group order shuffled with seed 42 + repeat index and dealt round-robin to folds. Inside every fold, using the training groups only: the pulse family, saturation level and release fraction are chosen from {3 families} x {6 levels} x {release fractions 0.05, 0.1, 0.2} by the lowest mean over the three ranges of the median absolute error (these choices have no fitted parameters; the held-out fold never enters), the ridge strength is chosen from {0.01, 0.1, 1, 10, 100} by leave-one-GROUP-out on the training groups (the inner grouped cross-validation), the ridge is fitted on the training groups, and the held-out fold is scored once. The plain method and the spline choose their own release fraction (and level) the same way. Pooled result: the median absolute error over all out-of-fold predictions of one repeat, averaged over the 10 repeats; interval by bootstrap resampling of groups on the pooled predictions of repeat 0 plus the spread (min to max) across the 10 repeats is reported separately.

**Settings for O, G and A (no re-tuning).** Triangle pulse, saturation level 0.7 (fill), release fraction 0.1, spline level chosen on G's tuning tests; ridge strength chosen and ridge fitted on that split's own tuning tests. Note: the settings were chosen on G's tuning groups; some A and O held-out tests were in G's tuning set, so only the ridge is new on those splits. R is the only split where every choice is repeated inside the folds.

**Failure-case table (+-16 g).** The ten tests with the largest absolute error after fill + ridge: make, model, test type, peak g (the front-end-filtered peak and the raw peak) and the share of pulse samples at or above the saturation level. Listed for the held-out set of G and, separately, from the out-of-fold predictions of R.

# FILE: ALGORITHMS_COMPLIANCE.md

# Algorithms compliance check

Generated by `python -m crashguard.compliance` from `results/results.json`. Status words: DONE, PARTIAL, NOT_DONE. A number that does not exist is printed as MISSING or NOT_RUN and is never filled in. The text of the original 'three algorithm contributions' prompt is not stored in the repository; the item list follows `docs/02_preregistration/ALGORITHMS.md` (written from it before the runs), the queue entry in `docs/07_log/PROGRESS.md` and the author's Part 0 list.

| Item | What | Status | Where (keys, files) | Number or note |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| R3.W | Two clean rebuilds agree; tests and ruff pass | **DONE** | scripts/run_all.ps1 -Clean (twice); scripts/compare_results.py | run 1 and run 2 agree on all but 35 of 88,185 entries; those are the Windows compiled-C checks, which Application Control blocked in run 1 (NOT_RUN with the reason) and allowed in run 2; `check_results` 0 errors in both; the piecemeal results of the session agree with run 1 on all but 32 entries (the same blocked checks) |

NOT_DONE items: A1.14. PARTIAL items: A1.5, A1.13, A2.8, W3, R3.S7, R3.S12, R3.B1, R3.C.
A1.14 cannot be made DONE: the primary variant was not named before the results. A1.5 describes a change that was logged. A1.13 and W3 depend on the ESP32 or on Windows allowing the compiled test program; A2.8 needs the ESP32.

# FILE: ARCHITECTURE.md

# Architecture

## System (hardware + software)
```
CAR NODE (ESP32)                                   BASE STATION (ESP32)
MPU6050 100 Hz ─┐                                  verify HMAC + counter
5x SW-420 ──────┼─> window (2.8 s) ─> 14 features  ─> signed ACK
NEO-6M GPS ─────┘      │                                │
                       ├─> rule engine (severity)        ├─> dashboard / ThingSpeak
                       ├─> RF model  (crash prob)        ├─> event log (dataset)
                       └─> direction (first 60 ms)       └─> responder alert
                  cancel window (5-10 s by severity)
                  sign (HMAC-SHA256/64 + counter) ─> LoRa 433 MHz, duty-cycle token bucket
```

## Software modules (`src/crashguard/`)
| Module | Responsibility | Input → Output |
|---|---|---|
| `data.py` | Download, verify, load VZCrash | HF dataset → `data/processed/events.parquet` |
| `features.py` | Windowing + 14 features | events → `features.parquet` |
| `split.py` | Group-held-out splits | features → split indices |
| `models.py` | Train and evaluate the five models over 5 seeds | features → `results/` |
| `export_c.py` | RF → C header, equivalence test | model → `firmware_export/` |
| `packet.py` | 97-byte alert + ACK encode/decode, HMAC | dict ↔ bytes |
| `verifier.py` | Reference base-station verifier, counter store | bytes → accept/reject |
| `attacks.py` | Attack suite | → `tables/attacks.csv` |
| `lora.py` | Airtime, duty-cycle budget | params → ms, alerts/h |
| `delivery_sim.py` | Event-driven delivery simulation | policy, loss → delivery stats |
| `results.py` | Single writer for `results.json` with origin labels | any → json |
| `plots.py` | Shared figure style | |
| `report.py` | Builds `report/software_results.md` | results.json → md |
| `merge/ingest_hardware.py` | Hardware CSV ingest + E1–E8 analysis | `data/hardware` → results |

## Rules for code
- Only `results.py` writes `results.json`. Each entry has the form `{"name","value","unit","origin","source_file","notes"}`.
- Analysis code must not hard-code numbers, except design constants from the specs (packet sizes, policy timings).

---

Generated by `python -m crashguard.attack_doc` from `results/tables/attacks.csv` and `results/results.json`. Do not edit by hand.

**Count (from `attacks.cases_total`): 48 cases, 48 behave as expected.** Origin of every row: SIMULATED (a software simulation of the reference verifier and the node's ACK check; not a measurement on the radios). Every other document takes this count from `results.json`; `tests/test_consistency.py` fails if a document states a different number.

| Id | Description | Expected | Observed | Origin | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| 01 | ACK length | 26 | 26 | SIMULATED |  |
| 02 | Valid alert | accept | accept | SIMULATED |  |
| 03 | Valid alert gets a valid ACK | True | True | SIMULATED |  |
| 04 | Forged tag | reject_tag | reject_tag | SIMULATED |  |
| 05 | Alert signed with the wrong key | reject_tag | reject_tag | SIMULATED |  |
| 06 | Tampered field (severity) | reject_tag | reject_tag | SIMULATED |  |
| 07 | Tampered field (counter) | reject_tag | reject_tag | SIMULATED |  |
| 08 | Tampered field (delta-V, bytes 38-39) | reject_tag | reject_tag | SIMULATED |  |
| 09 | Tampered field (ASI, bytes 40-41) | reject_tag | reject_tag | SIMULATED |  |
| 10 | Delta-V and ASI fields round-trip in the 97-byte alert | True | True | SIMULATED |  |
| 11 | Replay (same packet again): not processed again, same ACK re-sent | reack | reack | SIMULATED | an attacker only gets back the ACK that was already sent; no new alert, no state change |
| 12 | Replay leaves the base state unchanged | True | True | SIMULATED |  |
| 13 | Strict verifier (before the fix): ACK lost, car retries the same packet | reject_counter | reject_counter | SIMULATED | FOUND BY OUR TESTING: the car never gets its ACK; this is why the idempotent re-ACK was added |
| 14 | ACK lost, car retries same packet: re-ACKed | reack | reack | SIMULATED |  |
| 15 | The re-ACK is accepted by the car | True | True | SIMULATED |  |
| 16 | Old counter, different content: rejected | reject_counter | reject_counter | SIMULATED |  |
| 17 | Same counter, different content (event changed): rejected | reject_counter | reject_counter | SIMULATED |  |
| 18 | Replay of an alert older than the re-ACK window: rejected | reject_counter | reject_counter | SIMULATED |  |
| 19 | Retry of the same event with a fresh counter | accept | accept | SIMULATED |  |
| 20 | Forged alert does not advance the counter | True | True | SIMULATED |  |
| 21 | Wrong length (truncated) | reject_length | reject_length | SIMULATED |  |
| 22 | Wrong message type (alert with type 2) | reject_type | reject_type | SIMULATED |  |
| 23 | Wrong version | reject_version | reject_version | SIMULATED |  |
| 24 | Valid ACK fed to the base as an alert (reflection) | reject_length | reject_length | SIMULATED |  |
| 25 | Spoofed ACK (attacker key) | reject | reject | SIMULATED |  |
| 26 | Spoofed ACK (random tag) | reject | reject | SIMULATED |  |
| 27 | Old ACK replayed for a later alert | reject | reject | SIMULATED |  |
| 28 | Genuine ACK for the waiting alert | accept | accept | SIMULATED |  |
| 29 | Alert bytes presented as an ACK | reject | reject | SIMULATED |  |
| 30 | Base reboot, counter persisted: replay not processed again | reack | reack | SIMULATED |  |
| 31 | Base reboot, counter persisted: older replay rejected | reject_counter | reject_counter | SIMULATED |  |
| 32 | Base reboot, counter lost: replay | accept | accept | SIMULATED | VULNERABILITY (documented): a recorded alert is accepted again after the counter store is lost |
| 33 | Replay flood, no limit: the base keeps ACKing until its duty-cycle budget is gone | budget exhausted | budget exhausted | SIMULATED |  |
| 34 | Replay flood, no limit: a genuine alert arriving during the flood gets no ACK | accept_no_ack | accept_no_ack | SIMULATED | the flood starves the legitimate ACK: a denial of service on ACKs |
| 35 | Replay flood, with limit: at most one re-ACK per retry interval | True | True | SIMULATED |  |
| 36 | Replay flood, with limit: the base never runs out of budget | True | True | SIMULATED |  |
| 37 | Replay flood, with limit: a genuine alert arriving during the flood is ACKed | accept | accept | SIMULATED |  |
| 38 | Replay flood, with limit: re-ACK airtime is lower than without the limit | True | True | SIMULATED |  |
| 39 | Limit on: a retry inside the retry interval is not re-ACKed | reack_limited | reack_limited | SIMULATED |  |
| 40 | Limit on: a genuine retry one interval after the ACK is still re-ACKed | reack | reack | SIMULATED |  |
| 41 | Every single-bit flip of a valid alert is rejected | True | True | SIMULATED | the tag covers the body; the last bytes are the tag itself; all flips tried |

| 45 | Every single-bit flip of a valid ACK is rejected by the node | True | True | SIMULATED |  |
| 46 | Counter exhausted (largest value seen): a node whose counter wraps to 0 is rejected | reject_counter | reject_counter | SIMULATED | LIMIT (documented): after the 32-bit counter is used up the key must be rotated; there is no epoch field in this layout |
| 47 | Alert for another node_id signed with the shared key is accepted | accept | accept | SIMULATED | LIMIT (documented): one shared key, so anyone who holds it can sign for any node; there is no per-node key and no key rotation |

## Additional designed test cases (T11)

10 additional designed test cases written after the original cases, with the expected outcome fixed before the run; 10 behave as expected. These are DESIGNED TEST CASES, not independent attackers, and they are counted separately from the original cases above (`attacks_x.*` in `results.json`). Where the expected outcome documents a known weakness (X06, X07) the case is expected to show it.

| Id | Description | Category | Expected | Observed | Origin | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| X01 | Flash with the 16-bit counter wrapped to 0 after counter 65535 | counter rollover | accept_flash | accept_flash | SIMULATED | reconstructed as 65536, above the last accepted flash |
| X02 | Replay of an old flash (true counter 5) after the counter passed 65,536 | counter rollover | reject_counter | reject_counter | SIMULATED | reconstructed value is not above the last accepted flash |
| X03 | Random 8-byte tags on a valid alert body (200,000 tries, bounded budget) | tag forgery | 0 accepted | 0 accepted | SIMULATED | success probability per try 2^-64 (ANALYTICAL); expected successes about 1.1e-14 |
| X04a | Valid ACK arrives late, after the car retried, while it still waits for that alert | delayed delivery | accept | accept | SIMULATED |  |
| X04b | Valid ACK of alert 1 arrives after the car moved on to alert 2 | delayed delivery | reject | reject | SIMULATED |  |
| X05 | The same CANCEL delivered twice | CANCEL | cancelled,cancelled | cancelled,cancelled | SIMULATED | idempotent: the second copy changes nothing |
| X06 | CANCEL arrives before the alert it cancels | CANCEL | reject_unknown_event; event not cancelled | reject_unknown_event; event not cancelled | SIMULATED | KNOWN WEAKNESS documented by the model check: the early CANCEL is refused and the late alert stays active |
| X07 | Flash of a second node whose short id equals the first node's (counter 10 against 50) | identity | reject_counter | reject_counter | SIMULATED | KNOWN LIMIT: the 16-bit short id must be unique in the fleet; a collision refuses the second node |
| X08 | A 14-byte flash given to the alert handler and a 97-byte alert given to the flash handler | type confusion | reject_length,reject_length | reject_length,reject_length | SIMULATED |  |
| X09 | A valid ACK of the flash (event id 0xFFFFFFFF) presented to a node waiting for the alert of event 1 | ACK | reject | reject | SIMULATED |  |

# FILE: CLAIM_AUDIT.md

# Independent claim audit (T16, 2026-11-11)

**Method.** A separate pass (a subagent) read only `docs/03_results/CLAIM_EVIDENCE.md`, `report/novelty_summary.md` and `results/results.json`. It did not see the source code, the decision log, the preregistration or how the claims were produced. It checked wording, origin labels, quoted numbers, overstatement, exploratory flags and contradictions between the two documents. It changed nothing; every fix below was made by the author of the documents, to the wording only. No result was changed.

**Machine checks passed.** Every cited key exists, every origin label matches the `origin` field, every quoted value matches `results.json` to the printed precision (one 0.8 rounding in a flash estimate), and the words "first", "novel" and "state-of-the-art" do not appear.

**70 claims checked, 18 problems reported.** All 18 were checked against `results.json` before acting. Outcome:

| # | Finding | Action |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | HL title says "evaluated end to end" while the hardware row says NOT SUPPORTED | HL title now says the software parts are built and tested and the end-to-end evaluation has not been run |
| 2 | N16 "not better than a severity table" and the burst-simulator result disagree without naming the simulator | N16 title names both simulators and their scenario sets |
| 3 | N28 said H-a, H-b and H-c all hold; T6 said H-c survives all shifts (two fail); H-b's airtime part is PARTIAL | N28, the T6 row and the T14 row reworded |
| 4 | HL and N2 said "no software evidence for impact direction" | HL now cites the front-against-side check on NHTSA tests (N17) |
| 5 | SF "supports the transfer argument" while N23 shows the scale-free features are not invariant | SF title says it is a hypothesis and names the counter-evidence |
| 6 | N19 ranked row quoted seed-42 numbers and a SIMULATED label | now the 5-seed mean and MEASURED_SOFTWARE (timing read from the window length) |
| 7 | N22 and the report said the base ACK budget is never a bottleneck, "at most a few percent" | wrong at high alert rates (up to 0.35 at SF7 and 0.69 at SF12 of the allowance); report and N22 now give the numbers and restrict the claim to the default alert rate |
| 8 | N20 said weighted calibration was not tried; the ranked row used the non-de-duplicated rate; T7 widening margin contains alpha | N20 text, ranked row and T7 wording corrected; "not established" for the margin variant |
| 9 | SUPPORTED and PARTIAL applied differently to simulated and analytical rows | both legends now say what SUPPORTED means for a SIMULATED or ANALYTICAL row |
| 10 | Headline recall 0.900 without the 0.873 on the set without window-level near-duplicates | HL row and the draft paragraph give both (EXPLORATORY) |
| 11 | N14 "no gain at 32 and 64 g" while two intervals clear zero by under a point; tail and 80% target not shown in the claim | wording changed to "no practically meaningful gain"; tail and target already listed under "still missing" |
| 12 | N18 "adds nothing" while the partial association is small and negative with an interval below zero | wording now "no positive added association" |
| 13 | NHTSA high-speed barrier caveat missing from several rows | added to N13, N17, N18, N28 and as a footer to the claim table |
| 14 | N26 "does not help in steady state" holds only against the severity table; single-car result not flagged | wording and EXPLORATORY flags added; spreading-factor raise added to the components that matter |
| 15 | T3 and T8 rows stronger than the numbers (T8 "every cell": 16 of 24) | T3 wording limited to the current-threshold table; T8 row reworded to "most cells" |
| 16 | The C-equivalence row said "equals" while one synthetic N14 check fails | row says equals on the real inputs and names the failing synthetic check |
| 17 | N4: one scenario, no interval, lower all-alert reception not stated; duty limit row lacked "not verified for India" | added to N4 and the duty row |
| 18 | Small items: N25 recall and precision, N21 attack type, "33 NHTSA tests" (should be 176), N10 AP gap, a wildcard key | all corrected |

**Not changed.** The audit's point that N12 is SUPPORTED on simulation only is covered by the new legend. The 15-tree forest against the 56 KB recommended forest is already in the N10 evidence.

# FILE: CLAIM_EVIDENCE.md

# Claim evidence: each novelty point against the 24 project papers

Written 2026-11-11. Papers are the 24 PDFs in `docs/literature/` (text extracted with pdftotext into `docs/literature/_text/`, git-ignored). Paper 01 had no text layer and was read through OCR (RapidOCR), so its quotes may carry OCR errors. Papers are cited by file number (01 to 24, in alphabetical file order): 01 Kumar, Acharya and Lohani 2020, IoT accident detection and classification with sensor fusion on a smartphone (IEEE IoT J.); 02 dashcam audio ensemble 2021; 03 AI-enabled accident alert, smart cities 2022; 04 ML framework, multimodal sensors in cars 2022; 05 LoRa LPWAN search and rescue 2022; 06 secure accident reporting, ECIES 2023; 07 speaker identification and blockchain 2023; 08 IoT accident detection and visual situation 2024; 09 bi-level crash risk forecasting 2024; 10 AV sensor-related accident severity 2024; 11 calibrated confidence crash severity 2024; 12 federated near-crash detection 2024; 13 spatiotemporal CNN crash prediction 2025; 14 AI traffic crash severity 2025; 15 ensemble imbalance injury severity 2025; 16 real-time IoT public safety alerts 2025; 17 drones, ML and LoRa 2025; 18 crash simulation models for crash-detection calibration 2025; 19 vehicle-based disaster alerting, LoRa 2025; 20 to 24 statistical crash-severity studies 2026 (road, glare, construction, farm, SOM clustering, rural).

**Reading rule.** A zero-hit search shows absence only in these 24 papers. It says nothing about the wider literature. Searches beyond the 24 papers are in `docs/06_references/PRIOR_WORK.md` and `docs/06_references/MANUAL_SEARCH_CHECKLIST.md`.

## Term search over the 24 extracts (2026-11-11, case-insensitive)
| Term | Papers with a hit | What the hits are |
|---|---|---|
| HMAC | none | |
| replay | 07, 12 | 07: voice-replay spoofing. 12: replay attacks on a federated-learning scheme, not on packets |
| authentication | 05, 06, 07, 16, 17 | 06: lightweight cryptography survey and ECIES. 05: encryption plus an integrity check. 16: certificate-based mutual authentication for MQTT. None describe a counter or an ACK |
| duty cycle | none | |
| ACK / acknowledg | 08, 16 (protocol sense: none) | 08: an acknowledgement SMS sent to the person who reports. No protocol ACK in any paper |
| severity | 01, 03, 05, 08, 09, 10 to 24 | 10, 11, 14, 15, 20 to 24 are statistical severity studies on report data. 03, 08, 16, 17, 19 give a severity label in an alert (see rows below) |
| direction | many | Travel direction or camera direction. None estimates the side or angle of impact |
| microcontroller / ESP32 / Arduino | 01, 03, 04 (reference list), 05, 08, 16, 19 | Hardware used as a gateway to a phone or cloud; no on-device trained model reported |
| delta-V / velocity change | none | |
| ASI / severity index | none as Acceleration Severity Index | 07: MFCC feature name. 11, 14: a crash severity index variable |
| near-miss / near-crash | 02, 04, 09, 12, 21 | 02: dashcam audio with near-crash clips. 12: near-crash detection from video. Not IMU-only |
| duplicate / leakage | 04, 06, 08, 14, 16, 21 | 04: removes duplicates in preprocessing. 08: merges duplicate witness reports. None audits identical recordings across train and test splits |

Verdict codes: YES = a paper already does it; PARTLY = a paper does a related part; NOT FOUND = no hit in the 24 papers (not proof of absence).

| Point | Claim sentence | Closest paper(s) and place | Short quote | In the 24 papers | What is left that is ours |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| HL | The integrated system (microcontroller detector, impact direction, authenticated severity-aware LoRa delivery) is evaluated end to end | 05 (LoRa packet with encryption and a channel check); 16 (severity-scored alerts); 08 (IMU, vibration, MQTT report) | 05 p6, Algorithm 1 (RoboMaC Transmitter), steps 8 and 9: "if LoRa channel is free, then" / "Send the LoRa Packet" | PARTLY: each part appears in a different paper, none combines all | The combination, with the hardware part still not measured. PARTIAL until H1 to H6 |
| N1 | Real-crash-trained detector on a cheap MCU, with a transfer test to the scale model | 04 (ML on real SHRP2 crashes from in-car sensors); 01 (threshold classifier on a phone); 03, 08, 16 (microcontroller as gateway) | 04 p1: "detecting real-world driving accidents" | PARTLY: real-crash ML (04) is off-device; MCU papers use thresholds | Flash and RAM size against accuracy with C export checked against Python; the transfer test needs hardware |
| N2 | Impact side and angle from one IMU are carried in the alert | None in the 24 | | NOT FOUND | Outside the 24: Tadic and Vukajlovic 2016 estimate the principal direction of force from low-cost MEMS (see PRIOR_WORK). Software evidence for this point is not built. Stays NOT_YET |
| N3 | HMAC, anti-replay counter and signed ACK on the MCU, with the spoofed-ACK case | 05 (encrypts and checks integrity, no counter); 06 (ECIES over MQTT); 16 (TLS certificates) | 05 p6, Algorithm 1 steps 8 and 9: "if LoRa channel is free, then" / "Send the LoRa Packet"; 05 p6 (text, receiver): "the incoming data was hacked or otherwise corrupted" | PARTLY: integrity and encryption appear; counter, replay and signed ACK do not | Outside the 24, an ESP32 HMAC component with a counter exists (embedblocks msg_auth, opened) and LoRaWAN has counters. Ours: the 97-byte alert, 26-byte ACK, attack suite of <!--n:attacks.cases_total-->48<!--/n--> cases (`docs/01_protocol/ATTACK_CASES.md`), the retry fix. PARTIAL |
| N4 | Severity-aware delivery under a 10% duty-cycle budget | 16 (severity score routes alerts to three audiences); 05 (waits for a free channel) | 16 p19 table: "Severity-aware alerting" | PARTLY: severity-based routing exists (16), duty cycle absent | Retry and airtime policy by severity under a duty-cycle budget, simulated. Priority-aware LoRa scheduling exists outside the 24 (Osaka group, snippet only). PARTIAL |
| N5 | Measured stage-by-stage latency against HTTP, MQTT and CoAP | 16 (MQTT over TLS with LoRa fallback); 08 (MQTT) | 16 abstract: "secure MQTT over TLS, with fallback to LoRa" | NOT FOUND for a measured comparison | Needs the hardware logs. NOT_YET |
| N6 | Open labelled scale-model dataset | None | | NOT FOUND | Needs hw_events.csv. NOT_YET |
| N7 | Measured value of a cheap vibration-sensor array | 08 (SW-420 vibration in three levels) | 08 p5: "vibration has been categorized into 3 levels, namely, low, moderate, and high" | PARTLY: a vibration sensor is used with a level label, not measured against an IMU | Needs hardware. NOT_YET |
| N8 | Identical recordings appear across the official VZCrash splits | 04 (duplicates removed in preprocessing only) | 04: "eliminating any duplicates, irregularities in the data" | NOT FOUND as a split audit | The audit and the de-duplicated test set. SUPPORTED, a data-quality finding about one dataset, to be reported to its authors |
| N9 | Trigger-aware evaluation: system recall includes the trigger stage | 18 (calibrates a crash-detection trigger on crash pulses) | 18 p1: "crash-detection algorithm" calibration | PARTLY: trigger design is studied for restraint systems, not reported as system recall on a pre-triggered dataset | Metric definition and sweep on VZCrash. SUPPORTED as evaluation rigour |
| N10 | Size against accuracy on the MCU, with C-export equivalence | None in the 24 | | NOT FOUND | Depth and tree sweep with a validation-only choice. SUPPORTED |
| N11 | False-alarm rate on near_miss events alone (a breakdown) | 02, 12 (near-crash from audio and video) | | PARTLY: near-crash is studied with other sensors | A breakdown, not a contribution |
| N12 | Retry versus replay in a custom point-to-point link, with a bounded fix | None in the 24 | | NOT FOUND | Known LoRaWAN issue outside the 24 (ChirpStack forum, opened). Ours is a re-discovery with a bounded fix |
| N13 | Physics-based severity (delta-V, ASI, rollover) from one low-rate IMU, carried in the alert | 01 (rollover from roll or pitch above 90 degrees with a complementary filter; severity not measured); 08 (hit severity from vibration); 16 (composite severity score) | 01: "if either roll or pitch is greater than 90°, a rollover happened" (OCR) | PARTLY: rollover by angle (01) and severity labels (08, 16) exist; delta-V and ASI do not appear | Outside the 24: Tadic and Vukajlovic 2016 (delta-V and direction from consumer MEMS, low-G sensors saturated, opened). Ours: the range and rate study on NHTSA tests. PARTIAL |
| SF | Scale-free features keep most of the AP | None | | NOT FOUND | Supports the transfer argument only. PARTIAL |

## Notes on the closest papers
- **05 (LoRa search and rescue).** Packet is encrypted, then sent only if the channel is free; the receiver rejects data it finds hacked. No counter, no ACK, no duty-cycle analysis. This is the closest source for the security part, and the existing NOVELTY.md already lists it.
- **16 (public safety alerts).** Its table lists severity-aware alerting as a feature, using a weighted score and three escalation levels. The severity there is a fusion of environment, motion, biometric and video inputs, not delta-V. It means N4 and N13 cannot say "severity in an alert" is new. Only the physics basis and the airtime policy remain.
- **01 (Kumar et al. 2020).** It separates collision, fall-off and rollover with a complementary filter and says it does not focus on severity monitoring. It uses a 5 g and 23 km/h argument. This is the closest source for the rollover step, so a drift-corrected rollover flag is not new either.
- **18 (crash-detection calibration).** Restraint-system trigger calibration from crash pulses. Related to the trigger stage; it works on simulated and tested crash pulses, not on a pre-triggered fleet dataset.

## Quote re-check (round 3, S1.2)
Every quoted phrase attributed to one of the 24 papers was searched in the extracted text (`docs/literature/_text`, whitespace collapsed) and in a page-by-page extraction of the PDFs to confirm the page. Result:
| Quote | Paper and page found | Match |
|---|---|---|
| "if LoRa channel is free, then" / "Send the LoRa Packet" | 05, p6, Algorithm 1 steps 8 and 9 | exact (now the quoted text; the running-text sentence "If the channel is free, the encrypted data packet will be sent." is also exact on p6) |
| "the incoming data was hacked or otherwise corrupted" | 05, **p6** | exact text; the page was given as p5 before and is corrected |
| "detecting real-world driving accidents" | 04, p1 | exact |
| "eliminating any duplicates, irregularities in the data" | 04, p8 | exact |
| "Severity-aware alerting" | 16, p19 | exact |
| "secure MQTT over TLS, with fallback to LoRa" | 16, p1 | exact |
| "vibration has been categorized into 3 levels, namely, low, moderate, and high" | 08, p5 | exact |
| "crash-detection algorithm" | 18, p1 (also pp2, 3, 23, 26) | exact |
| "if either roll or pitch is greater than 90 degrees, a rollover happened" | 01 (OCR) | matches only after OCR spacing is restored (the OCR has no spaces between words); wording otherwise as the OCR reads |

Not checkable here: "strictly separated by vehicle" (VZCrash paper, arXiv 2606.06074) is not among the 24 papers and its PDF is not kept in this repository; the wording was copied when the paper was read on 2026-11-11 and has not been re-checked against a stored copy. The phrases "severity in an alert", "no security" and "built, not yet measured" are our own wording, not quotes.

## Part 0B literature additions (2026-11-11)
Full details, with exactly what was opened for each paper, are in `docs/06_references/PRIOR_WORK.md` ("Part 0B literature entries"). They are numbered L1 to L9 here and are NOT among the 24 project papers, so the zero-hit reading rule above does not apply to them.

| Ref | Source | Opened as | What it shows | Novelty points touched |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| L1 | VZCrash paper, arXiv 2606.06074 | full text | Splits "strictly separated by vehicle" (72/14/14, Section III-B); on-device threshold pre-selection (Section II); AP baselines 97.56 (CNN-RNN), 97.54 (CNN-Transformer), 89.62 (peak-acceleration baseline); no tree models; no embedded test; direction and severity labels listed as future work (Section IV); 143 crashes among 735,000 real-world events (under 0.02%), best-model AP falls to about 86% (Section III-D); no licence in the paper | N1, N8, N9, SF, X8 dropped |
| L2 | Balfaqih et al. 2022, Sustainability 14(1) 210 | abstract only | Microcontroller classifier of severity (GMM, NB tree, DT, CART), crash sides and severity sent by GSM. Arduino, RC-car data and "no security" NOT verified | N1, N2, N13 |
| L3 | Katsidimas et al. 2023, Sensors 23(2) 896 | full text (Europe PMC) | Random forest on an Arduino Nano 33 BLE with flash (88 KB) and RAM (162 KB) reported; public Zenodo dataset; plate impacts with PZT sensors | N1, N10 |
| L4 | Ebert et al. 2016, arXiv 1703.05680 | full PDF | Impact segment and direction from one phone IMU on an RC car; four diagonal classes | N2, N6 |
| L5 | Zhong and Nie 2024, Sci Rep 14 (article number unconfirmed; DOI 10.1038/s41598-024-59058-8) | full text (Europe PMC) | Confirmed LoRa messaging with local ACK and at most 2 retransmissions; no replay, duty-cycle or priority text | N3, N4, N12 |
| L6 | Tomic et al. 2020, arXiv 2002.01472 | full PDF | LoRaWAN duty-cycle blackout periods for event-triggered traffic; priority slots only as future work | N4, N16, X6 |
| L7 | Wei et al. 2023, Sensors 23(5) 2660 | full text (Europe PMC) | Priority-based spreading-factor allocation under 1% duty cycle; no retries, no token budget | N4, N16, X6 |
| L8 | Chan et al. 2023, Sensors 23(10) 4609 (algorithm of Ruder et al.) | full text (Europe PMC) | 5th-order spline through 3 points each side of a clipped segment; still underestimates peaks (-1.40 +- 4.53 g mean, worse for higher peaks) | N14 |
| L9 | Tadic and Vukajlovic 2016, Radioengineering 25(1) | opened earlier | Delta-V and PDOF from consumer MEMS in one real test; saturation of low-G sensors | N2, N13, N14, X1 |

**Downgrades from this reading (also in `report/novelty_summary.md` and `docs/06_references/NOVELTY.md`):**
- **N2** (impact side and angle): a supporting feature, not a contribution. L4 (phone IMU, RC car), L2 (crash side by GSM) and L9 (PDOF from MEMS) exist.
- **N6** (open scale-model dataset): weak. RC-car data (L4) and open impact datasets (L3, on a plate) already exist, so only a labelled CrashGuard-specific set would be new, and it is not built.
- **N1** (real-crash-trained detector on a cheap MCU): partial. A random forest on a microcontroller with flash and RAM reported has precedent (L3, plates) and a CART severity classifier on a microcontroller is reported (L2, abstract only). The VZCrash paper (L1) tested no tree models and ran no embedded test; that is the gap the forest is evaluated in.
- **Integrated system (HL):** each part has a precedent (L2, L3, L5, L6, L7 and the 24 papers); the combination is unmeasured on hardware, so the wording stays "built, not yet measured".
- **N14:** L8 is the spline baseline; the comparison is in `docs/02_preregistration/ALGORITHMS.md` (N14 extension) and the novelty file.

**Still title-only or snippet-only (not used for any claim):** US 9,162,642; SAE 2015-01-1448; ChirpStack threads other than the one opened; the automated-crash-notification PDOF study; the PMC paper on sensor bandwidth and sample rate for head-impact sensors. (US 7,643,921 and the SAE 2025-01-5046 abstract page were read, see PRIOR_WORK.md.)

SUPPORTED = the claim as worded has software evidence and meets any rule fixed in advance (for a SIMULATED or ANALYTICAL row it holds in that simulation or model, not in the field); PARTIAL = part of the claim fails, or it rests on a simulation with assumed values that the claim goes beyond; NOT SUPPORTED = tried and not cleared, or no evidence. No hardware result is used. Rows about delta-V classes (N14, N24, H-a to H-c, T1 to T3, T6) use NHTSA crash tests, mostly high-speed barrier tests, which are not representative of all real crashes. Rows from the T block marked EXPLORATORY were not preregistered. The v2 rows (preregistered in docs/02_preregistration/PREREGISTRATION_V2.md) also use MIXED = one part of a two-part criterion holds, or the criterion holds but the Holm-adjusted p-value is not below 0.05.

## Allowed wording for the v2 claims (generated)

| Hypothesis | Verdict | Allowed wording | Not allowed |
|---|---|---|---|
| H-A1 | **SUPPORTED** | On 176 NHTSA crash tests, at +-16 g and 100 Hz, a quantile boosting model on clipping descriptors and pulse-family features has a 90th-percentile delta-V error of 42.4% against 58.5% for fill + ridge on repeated grouped cross-validation (difference 16.1 [9.3 to 23.4] points); the gain is 1.5 points on the make/model hold-out split A (interval 0.0 to 28.4); of the three fixed splits only the original-rule split O has its whole interval above zero. | That the estimator works on a real sensor or on crashes unlike the NHTSA tests; that it is the best estimator (plain gradient boosting has a lower 90th percentile on split R, EXPLORATORY; it is not shown better than the triangle fit alone or than ridge on all features); that it fixes the large-error tail. |
| H-A2 | **NOT SUPPORTED** | Normalised-score intervals bring coverage in the lowest clipped-fraction tercile to 0.90 (plain: 0.81) by being wider there (median relative width 0.99 against 0.75); they are narrower in the middle and highest terciles (0.38 and 0.52 against 0.74 and 0.77); the tests that never clip are covered 0.05 of the time. | That adaptive intervals are narrower or tighter where clipping is mild. |
| H-A3 | **NOT SUPPORTED** | At +-16 g the plain estimate is above the reference delta-V for 2 of 176 tests (but for 16 at +-32 g and 20 at +-64 g, so it is not a guarantee); the interval from the plain estimate to the pulse-family upper bound contains the reference for 0.55 of tests. | Guaranteed bounds; an upper bound on delta-V from the unclipped part of the pulse. |
| H-B1 | **NOT SUPPORTED** | In the simulator, none of five load-adaptive escalation variants raised critical reach within 10 s by 3 points over a matched fixed lowered-threshold rule in the burst cells (best mean gain 0.0080); the beacon variant lowered it in cells (for example -0.077 [-0.096 to -0.059] in the loaded SF9 cell). No tested variant reached a 3-point gain in these simulated cells. | That adaptive escalation improves delivery; that where escalations go matters (not shown). |
| H-B2 | **MIXED** | The largest point harm of any variant in a steady or single-car cell is 0.036 (graceful demotion, single car at 60% loss); its interval includes 2 points. For no variant does a whole interval of harm lie above 2 points, but some upper 95% bounds do (beacon variant in the steady cell with loss 0.3, demotion in the single-car cell); two variants are worse than their baseline with the whole interval below zero in some burst cells. | That adaptive variants are safe in steady state. |
| H-B3 | **NOT SUPPORTED** | Not evaluable because H-B1 is not met (the token bucket's mean gain is 0.0003 against 0.0080 for the best variant). | Any claim that a simple rule suffices. |
| H-D1 | **NOT SUPPORTED** | Among the Severe or Critical tests it flags (0.56 of them), the clip-onset flag saves a median of 86 ms (interval 80 to 95); the running delta-V threshold flags 0.69 and saves 55 ms, so the flag is earlier where it fires but misses 0.13 more of the Severe or Critical tests. | That the clip onset gives an early severity flag of 100 ms or more. |
| H-D2 | **MIXED** | The clip-onset flag has an over-triage of 0.054 [0.008 to 0.115]; the raw p-value is 0.055 (Holm-adjusted 0.385) and the interval reaches 0.115. | That the over-triage is below 10% with confidence. |
| H-E1 | **NOT SUPPORTED** | Dropping the tag from 8 to 4 bytes changes critical reach within 10 s by 0.0086 [0.0035 to 0.0136] in the simulated burst cells (about one point). | That a shorter tag gives a capacity gain of 2 points or more. |
| H-E2 | **SUPPORTED** | With a truncated HMAC and a secret key, an 8-byte tag needs about 2^44 forgery attempts for a probability of 1e-6 (a count of attempts, not capped by the duty limit); the smallest tag with both work factors above 2^32 is 7 bytes. The duty limit does not bind an attacker. | A cryptographic proof; that the key cannot be recovered. |
| H-F1 | **NOT SUPPORTED** | In the cost model a positive Critical hold is optimal when D / C_false is below 52 at the precision of the deployed forest at 0.02% prevalence. | That a zero Critical hold is optimal for any cost ratio. |
| H-F2 | **SUPPORTED** | In the simulator, a 2 s hold of Severe alerts reduces the share of false Severe alerts reaching the base by a relative 0.32 [0.25 to 0.39]; critical reach within 10 s changes by -0.008 [-0.029 to 0.010] (a loss of up to 2.9 points is not excluded); the delay to true Severe alerts is not measured. | That the hold is free in the field (the cancel model and every cost are assumptions). |

Studies A and D use 176 NHTSA tests (mostly high-speed barrier tests, not representative of all real crashes); B, H-E1 and F2 are simulations with assumed values, H-E2 and F1 are formulas; the 1% duty limit is not verified for India. Study C is NOT_RUN (see `docs/03_results/V2_RESULTS.md`).

At +-16 g fill + ridge has a median error of 18.0% against 16.7% for the spline (paired difference 1.31 [-1.41 to 4.37] points). | That the N13 or N14 findings hold for other crash modes; that fill + ridge beats the spline outside frontal and left-side tests. |

On the held-out modes no configuration does. | That a part will perform so on a vehicle (software sensor model; datasheet rates, not ESP32 read rates; mostly high-speed barrier tests). |

| V4 | For the 15-tree forest, removing the window-level duplicates lowers system recall by 0.0280 [0.0235 to 0.0326] and recall at the 1% target by 0.0727 [0.0641 to 0.0809]; removing exact copies only changes them by 0.0006 [-0.0008 to 0.0018] and 0.0045 [0.0024 to 0.0067]. | That the duplicates explain all of the gap to a clean evaluation (vehicle-level leakage cannot be checked). |
| H-X1 | Quantising thresholds and leaves of the 10-tree forest to 16 bits changes AP by 0.000000 (upper 95% bound 0.00002); the host flash estimate falls by 22.2% in the existing node layout (44.4% if the left child is implicit). | That the flash target of 30% is met with the existing layout; any ESP32 compile size. |
| H-X2 | At a non-crash trigger rate not above that of 2 g, the cascade passes 0.964 [0.958 to 0.969] of crashes against 0.964 [0.958 to 0.969] for the 2 g threshold; the rule chosen is the 2 g threshold itself in most held-out categories. | That a cheap running-feature trigger beats the fixed threshold on VZCrash. |
| H-X3 | Down-weighting duplicate training windows changes recall at the 1% target on the strict set by 0.0011 [-0.0022 to 0.0045]; 19.0% of training windows are in a duplicate group. | That duplicates in training explain the gap between the supplied and the strict set. |
| H-X5 | At +-16 g and 100 Hz the Spearman correlation of fill + ridge with the reference delta-V is 0.751 [0.678 to 0.805], below the plain estimate's 0.841 [0.791 to 0.875]; at +-32 g it is 0.919 [0.887 to 0.941] and at +-64 g 0.966 [0.949 to 0.979]. Class agreement of fill + ridge at +-16 g: 0.717 [0.649 to 0.779]. | That a +-16 g sensor preserves the order of severity; any claim about injury in real crashes. |
| H-X6 | At +-16 g on split R the 90th-percentile error of the anchored estimator is 27.5% against 58.5% for fill + ridge (gain 31.0 [20.2 to 37.8] points); the gain has its interval above zero on split A and not on G or O; the anchored estimate is never below the plain estimate and over-corrects the 2 tests where plain exceeds the reference. | That it has a lower median error than the tail-aware model (8.9% against 8.5%), that the gain holds on every split, or that it works on a real sensor. |
| H-X7 | None of the 16 preregistered pairs and two fusion rules reaches a lower end of 0.80 (0 of 32 combinations meet it); a wider clipped-sample mask, defined after the initial result (exploratory), reaches it for 3 combinations where neither part alone does. | That fusing a +-16 g and a +-200 g part works as a general method; any time-synchronised or mounted result. |
| H-X8 | At the 1% target the sequential rule decides 120 [40 to 200] ms earlier at the median than the early-exit rule, with recall 0.0081 [0.0029 to 0.0135] higher; at 5% the difference is -20 [-120 to -20] ms (positive = the sequential rule is earlier) and recall -0.0015 [-0.0020 to -0.0009]. The recall gain over the single forest is an ensemble effect. | That the sequential rule is faster at both targets. |

# FILE: CLAUDE_CODE_PROMPT.md

# Prompt for Claude Code — CrashGuard software pipeline

Paste everything below the line into Claude Code, started in an empty project folder.

---

You are building the complete **software and data-science part** of CrashGuard, an ESP32-based vehicle crash detection and LoRa alert system (B.Tech final project, journal-paper target). I will merge your outputs with hardware measurements I collect separately. Work autonomously, step by step, and keep going until every deliverable below exists and passes its checks.

## 0. Non-negotiable rules

1. **Never invent results.** Every number in any table, figure or text must come from code you ran in this session. If something cannot be run (no data, no hardware), write `NOT RUN` and say why. No placeholder metrics, no "typical" values.
2. **Label every result's origin** in the output files: `MEASURED_SOFTWARE` (computed from a dataset), `SIMULATED` (from a simulation with stated assumptions), `ANALYTICAL` (from a formula), or `NOT_RUN`. Hardware results are not yours to produce.
3. **Fixed seeds** (seed = 42, plus 5 repeated seeds for any stochastic comparison) and a `requirements.txt` with pinned versions. Everything reproducible from one command: `make all`.
4. **Split by vehicle/trip, never by random window.** If the dataset has no vehicle or trip ID, say so in the report and use the most independent grouping available (for example by device or by time block), and state this limitation.
5. Write in simple, plain language in all reports. State limitations honestly. Do not say "first" or "novel" in any generated text.
6. Dataset licence is CC BY-NC 4.0: non-commercial use only, cite the source. Put this in the README.

## 1. Dataset

- **Dataset (Hugging Face):** https://huggingface.co/datasets/vzc-research-chapter/VZCrash
- **Paper:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06074 (PDF: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.06074)
- What I believe about it (from my notes, **verify before relying on it**): about 190,000 events, 31,090 verified crashes; IMU at 100 Hz in units of g; axes X forward, Y left, Z up; binary crash / non-crash labels; no impact-direction or severity labels.

Steps:
1. Download with `huggingface_hub` / `datasets` into `data/raw/` (not committed to git). If access fails, stop and tell me exactly what failed and what I should download by hand; do not substitute fake data.
2. Read the dataset card and the paper. Write `docs/dataset_facts.md` listing: file format, columns, units, sampling rate, window length, label definition, any vehicle/trip/device ID, class balance, and licence. Mark each fact `CONFIRMED_FROM_DATA` or `CONFIRMED_FROM_CARD/PAPER`. If anything differs from my beliefs above, say so clearly at the top of the file.
3. Run data-quality checks (missing values, constant signals, outliers, duplicated events, sampling-rate consistency) and save a short summary.

## 2. Features and models

1. Build fixed windows around the trigger (target 2.8 s at 100 Hz; adapt to what the dataset provides and document it). Compute **14 window features** and document each one in `docs/features.md` (suggested: peak |a|, peak along each axis, RMS, jerk peak, energy, duration above threshold, zero-crossings, kurtosis, post-impact quiet level, and similar). Mark which features are **scale-free** (independent of absolute g level, for example ratios and normalised shapes) as the `scale_free` subset.
2. Train and compare, on vehicle/trip-held-out splits:
   - Random forest, 15 trees, max depth 6 (the model that must fit on an ESP32).
   - Larger random forest (for reference).
   - Logistic regression.
   - Gradient-boosted trees (LightGBM or sklearn).
   - A simple rule-based detector (peak threshold plus duration), tuned on the training split only.
3. Metrics on the held-out test set: average precision, precision, recall, F1, false-positive rate at a fixed recall of 0.95, ROC-AUC, confusion matrix, precision-recall curve. Report mean ± std over 5 seeds. Choose the decision threshold on a validation split, never on the test set.
4. Run an ablation: all 14 features vs the `scale_free` subset only.
5. Error analysis: list the 20 most confident false positives and false negatives (event IDs only, plus the feature values) and describe common patterns in plain language.
6. Calibration check (reliability curve) for the 15-tree model.

## 3. Microcontroller export and footprint

1. Export the 15-tree depth-6 model to **plain C** (`firmware_export/crashguard_rf.h`): no dynamic memory, integer or float thresholds in `const` arrays, one `predict_prob(const float features[14])` function.
2. Verify the C code gives **identical** predictions to the Python model on the whole test set (compile with gcc, run, compare). Report the maximum difference.
3. Estimate flash and RAM from the compiled size (`size` output) and the number of nodes. These are `ANALYTICAL` or host estimates, not ESP32 measurements. Say so.
4. Create a tiny Arduino sketch `firmware_export/benchmark_rf.ino` that I can flash to the ESP32 to time `predict_prob` over 1,000 calls and print microseconds. Do not report any ESP32 timing yourself.

## 4. Alert protocol and security (software-only checks)

1. Implement in Python, with unit tests, the 97-byte alert packet and the signed ACK: fields for node ID, event ID, monotonic counter, timestamp, severity, impact side, angle, latitude, longitude, peak g, model probability, and a 64-bit truncated HMAC-SHA256 tag. Document the byte layout in `docs/packet_format.md`.
2. Write an **attack test suite** that sends these cases to a Python reference verifier and records accepted/rejected: valid, forged tag, tampered field, replayed packet, old counter, spoofed ACK, base reboot with counter persisted, base reboot with counter lost. Save the table. This validates the **design**, not the firmware; label it `SIMULATED`.

## 5. LoRa airtime and delivery simulation

1. Implement the Semtech LoRa airtime formula. Parameters are inputs (SF, bandwidth, coding rate, preamble, header, CRC). Default SF7, 125 kHz, CR 4/5, preamble 8, explicit header, CRC on. Reproduce: a 97-byte payload at SF7 should be about 169 ms; if it is not, find out why.
2. Compute the maximum alerts per hour under a 10% duty-cycle limit for SF7–SF12 (`ANALYTICAL`).
3. Simulate the severity-aware delivery policy over a range of packet loss probabilities (0–60%) with a token-bucket duty-cycle limiter:
   - Low: cancel window 10 s, 3 tries then stop.
   - Moderate: 10 s, retries for about 2.5 min.
   - Severe: 10 s, retry until ACK.
   - Critical: 5 s, retry until ACK.
   - Heartbeats use only spare airtime budget.
   Compare against a naive policy (fixed 3 retries, no priorities). Report delivery rate, time to ACK, and whether critical alerts still get through when the budget is nearly used up. The loss model is an assumption: state it, and label all results `SIMULATED`. These curves will later be compared with my measured hardware delivery rates.

## 6. Hardware-merge interface

I will supply hardware logs later. Make this easy:
1. Define the expected CSV schemas in `docs/hardware_schema.md` and write `merge/ingest_hardware.py` that reads them if present and prints `NOT_RUN` if absent. Schemas:
   - `hw_events.csv`: event_id, true_label, true_side, raw window columns (t_ms, ax, ay, az, gx, gy, gz), sw_fl, sw_fr, sw_ml, sw_mr, sw_rl, rule_sev, ml_prob, classify_us, ml_us, verify_us, airtime_ms, ack_rtt_ms, trigger_to_ack_ms, retries, rssi, snr, distance_m.
   - `hw_protocols.csv`: protocol, latency_ms, throughput_bps, packet_bytes, loss_pct.
2. When `hw_events.csv` is present, the script must run: the transfer test (train on VZCrash, test on my data, all features vs `scale_free`), the crash/no-crash metrics, the 5-class impact-side confusion matrix and angle error, severity vs peak-g correlation, delivery rate by distance and severity, the latency breakdown, and the SW-420 ablation (false alarms and misses with vs without vibration confirmation). Test the script on a **clearly labelled synthetic dummy file** kept in `tests/dummy/`, and make sure no output from the dummy file can be mistaken for a real result (watermark every figure from it with "DUMMY DATA").

## 7. Outputs

```
crashguard-software/
  README.md                 how to run, licence and citation, limitations
  Makefile                  make data / features / train / export / security / lora / report / all
  requirements.txt
  src/  tests/  merge/  firmware_export/  docs/
  results/
    results.json            every number, each with its origin label
    tables/*.csv            one CSV per table
    figures/*.png and *.pdf (dpi 300, colour-blind-safe, axes labelled with units)
  report/
    software_results.md     plain-language results section for the paper
```

Figures to produce: class balance; precision-recall curves for all models; confusion matrix for the 15-tree model; feature importance; all-features vs scale-free comparison; calibration curve; model size vs performance; LoRa airtime vs payload and SF; alerts per hour at 10% duty; delivery rate vs packet loss for the policy vs naive; time to ACK by severity; the attack test table as an image.

`report/software_results.md` must contain: what was run, the tables, the figures with captions, a section "What these results do and do not show", and a section "Still needed from hardware".

## 8. Checks before you finish

- `make all` runs from a clean checkout; all tests pass.
- Every number in `report/software_results.md` appears in `results/results.json` with the same value.
- No file contains a metric labelled as measured that was not computed by code in the repo.
- The C model matches Python predictions exactly (or you report the difference).
- Give me a final summary: what ran, what did not, anything about the dataset that contradicted my assumptions, and the single most important thing I should check by hand.

Work in small, committed steps. If you hit a decision that could change the science (for example, the dataset has no vehicle IDs), choose the most conservative option, write it in `docs/decisions.md`, and continue.

---

# FILE: DATA_AND_CODE_STATEMENT.md

# Data and code statement

Software-only results. The author's hardware logs (`data/hardware/`) were not read for any result here.

## Licence split (read this before the tables)

- **Code** (`src/`, `tests/`, `scripts/`, `Makefile`, `crashclip/`, `merge/`): Apache License 2.0, copyright 2026 Mritula S (`LICENSE`, `NOTICE`). The licence covers code only.
- **VZCrash-derived artefacts** (the surrogate-generator parameters of block F0, audit lists, event hashes and identifiers): CC BY-NC 4.0 with attribution to the VZCrash authors (Bianconcini et al., arXiv 2606.06074). They carry their own licence file in the folder that holds them.
- **Data of third parties** keep their own terms and are not redistributed here (table below).
- No DOI exists for this repository. `CITATION.cff` and `.zenodo.json` hold metadata only.

## Datasets

| Dataset | Used for | Licence | Redistributed here? |
|---|---|---|---|
| VZCrash (Bianconcini et al., arXiv 2606.06074; Hugging Face `vzc-research-chapter/VZCrash`) | detector, duplicate audit, leakage sensitivity, X1 to X4 and X8, surrogate generator | **CC BY-NC 4.0.** Author reply of 2026-11-12 (user-supplied statement, recorded in `docs/07_log/DECISIONS.md`): the dataset is released under CC BY-NC 4.0, the downloaded copy corresponds to Hugging Face revision `42e1482d005c0e545379d6cfa4939486c9fe4249`, and the authors allow event identifiers or hashes to be cited in a paper. Non-commercial use; cite the paper. | **No.** `data/raw/` is git-ignored. `results/paper/vz_audit_cross_split_groups.csv`, `results/tables/vz_audit_*.csv` and `release/vzcrash_audit_for_authors/` hold event identifiers, split and class labels, vehicle-size labels and one-way SHA-256 hashes; they hold no samples and are CC BY-NC 4.0. |
| NHTSA Vehicle Crash Test Database (public API, https://nrd.api.nhtsa.dot.gov/nhtsa/vehicle/swagger-ui/index.html) | N13, N14, T13a, T13c, sensor selection, X5 to X7, synthetic pulse generator fits | The data.gov catalogue entry was opened and its rights field reads "public". The nhtsa.gov terms pages returned HTTP 403, so **the NHTSA terms are not verified**. The data keep their own terms; the Apache-2.0 licence does not apply to them. | **No raw files.** The ISO MME zips and metadata are cached in `data/raw/nhtsa/` (git-ignored). Derived tables list test numbers, make, model, year and computed values (`results/tables/`). The 5,000-pulse synthetic file is generated, not NHTSA data (it is fitted to summary ranges of NHTSA pulses). |
| Accelerometer data sheets (Analog Devices, InvenSense/TDK, STMicroelectronics, Kionix/ROHM) | sensor table | Manufacturers' documents; copyright of their owners. | **No.** Local copies in `data/raw/refs/` are git-ignored; the repository stores the values read, with address, date and revision (`docs/05_data_code/SENSOR_DATASHEETS.md`, `docs/06_references/REFERENCES.md`). |

## What is released

Code (Apache-2.0), generated tables and figures (`results/`), documents (`docs/`), and `results/results.json` with the origin label of every number (MEASURED_SOFTWARE, SIMULATED, ANALYTICAL, HARDWARE, NOT_RUN). Citation: `CITATION.cff` (metadata only; no DOI is claimed).

## How to rebuild

`docs/05_data_code/REPRODUCE_FINISH.md` (one command per table and figure) and `docs/05_data_code/REPRODUCE.md` (full pipeline). Seeds: 42, and 0 to 4 for repeated runs. Pinned versions: `requirements.txt` and `requirements.lock`.

## Not available or not done

See `docs/07_log/NOT_RUN.md` (every NOT_RUN item with its reason, and every source that could not be opened).
